# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-29

## Embodied AI

| **[Zooming without Zooming v2: From Seeing Details to Connecting Evidence for Generalizable Multimodal Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37037v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal perception forms the foundation of multimodal intelligence. In particular, spatial perception and multimodal long-context understanding are two pivotal tasks that underpin embodied AI and multimodal agents. However, synthesizing targeted, high-quality training data for these tasks remains difficult and costly. Our key insight is that these tasks share an underlying capability, which we call connecting evidence: identifying relevant visual facts, binding them to the correct entities, recovering their relations, and composing them into an answer. Crucially, targeted training data are far easier to synthesize for this capability than for these tasks themselves. To strengthen this capability, we propose EvidenceConnect, an easy-to-implement data synthesis framework that uses zoomed views of related regions within a single image to generate questions requiring comparison, aggregation, or chaining of visual evidence. To ensure quality, a reference answer is retained only when independent VLMs agree on it, and region-ablation tests discard questions whose answer survives the removal of selected regions. We then train base VLMs on the resulting data with RLVR, obtaining Zooming without Zooming v2 (ZwZ2). Although the synthesized data are not tailored to any downstream benchmark, ZwZ2 improves fine-grained and spatial perception, with further gains on embodied spatial reasoning, multimodal long-context understanding, multimodal agent tasks, and general visual reasoning. These results establish EvidenceConnect as an effective framework for supervising evidence connection and suggest that strengthening this shared capability leads to more generalizable multimodal perception.</p></details> |  |
| **[AmaraSpatial-10K: A Spatially and Semantically Aligned 3D Dataset for Spatial Computing and Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23018v3)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Web-scale 3D asset collections are abundant but rarely ready to ingest without cleanup, suffering from arbitrary metric scaling, incorrect pivots, brittle geometry, and incomplete textures, defects that limit their use in embodied AI, robotics, and spatial computing. We present AmaraSpatial-10K, a dataset of 10,071 synthetic 3D assets standardised for direct ingestion. Each asset ships as a metric-scaled, deterministically anchored .glb with separated PBR maps, a convex collision hull, a paired reference image, and multi-sentence text metadata. Alongside the dataset we introduce a reusable evaluation suite for 3D asset banks, a continuous Scale Plausibility Score (SPS), a keyword-based Concept Density metric, anchor-error auditing, and a cross-modal CLIP coherence protocol, and apply it to AmaraSpatial-10K alongside matched subsets of Objaverse, HSSD, ABO, and GSO. We check the annotations independently of the LLM/VLM pipeline that produced them: measured ABO object heights fall inside our scale intervals 88.7-90.7% of the time, and three non-author raters auditing a stratified sample of 314 assets judge 95.5% plausibly scaled and 99.7% correctly anchored. AmaraSpatial-10K improves CLIP Recall@5 by 3.4x over Objaverse (0.612 vs. 0.181, median rank 267 -> 3), achieves a 99.1% settling-stability rate under Habitat-Sim in static placement and collision tests, with ~20x lower per-load wall time, and as a drop-in Holodeck asset bank improves planned-scale matching and is preferred by blinded human raters in 27 of 40 paired scenes. Controlled ablations on the same asset bank attribute the retrieval gain to description richness.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations and Datasets Track)</p></details> |
| **[Labference: Benchmarking the Reasoning-Action Connection in Robotic Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35252v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realizing Embodied AI for Science requires robots to translate experimental goals into reliable physical procedures, reducing hazardous and repetitive human work while generating evidence for scientific discovery. Although language-based planning and learned manipulation provide complementary capabilities, their integration requires robots to adapt subsequent operations to actual execution outcomes. For example, an unsuccessful placement changes what must be done next, even when the experimental goal remains unchanged. Existing benchmarks assess planning, manipulation, and laboratory workflows through task completion and reasoning or execution metrics; however, aggregate performance leaves the connection between outcome-dependent choices and physical execution unresolved. To address this evaluation gap, we introduce Labference, a benchmark for evaluating how observed physical outcomes guide subsequent choices and how those choices translate into robotic laboratory actions. Its evaluation protocol is designed to test outcome-dependent behavioral adaptation through controlled evidence variations, while independently measuring operation selection, physical execution, and task completion. Matched Oracle-assisted continuations from shared checkpoints then assess which forms of support improve subsequent choices and task completion. Initial weighing evaluations reveal target-selection errors in the tested VLA policies despite successful object transfers, whereas GPT-driven control achieves correct selection but encounters execution failures and substantial computation costs. Together, these findings highlight the need to connect task-appropriate reasoning with reliable, timely action. By making this connection measurable and diagnosable, Labference supports targeted improvements toward autonomous experimentation for Embodied AI for Science.</p></details> |  |
| **[Closing the empirical loop: autonomous AI agents conduct end-to-end research with human participants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13421v3)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Artificial intelligence systems are transforming scientific discovery by accelerating specific research tasks, from protein structure prediction to materials design, yet remain confined to narrow domains requiring substantial human oversight. Exponential growth of scientific literature and increasing domain specialisation constrain researchers capacity to synthesise knowledge across disciplines and develop unifying theories, motivating exploration of more general-purpose AI systems for science. Here we show that a domain-agnostic, agentic AI system can independently navigate the scientific workflow, from hypothesis generation through data collection to manuscript preparation. The system autonomously designed and executed three psychological studies on visual working memory, mental rotation, and imagery vividness, executed online data collection with 288 participants, developed analysis pipelines through over 8-hour continuous coding sessions, and produced completed manuscripts. The results demonstrate the capability of AI scientific discovery pipelines to conduct research with theoretical reasoning and methodological rigour comparable to experienced researchers, though with limitations in conceptual nuance and theoretical interpretation. This is a step toward embodied AI that can test hypotheses through real-world experiments, accelerating discovery by autonomously exploring regions of scientific space that human cognitive and resource constraints might otherwise leave unexplored. It raises important questions about the nature of scientific understanding and attribution of scientific credit.</p></details> |  |
| **[EAST: Embodied AI Safety Testing via Systematic Fault Injection in Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34358v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Task success is not a sufficient measure of safety for embodied artificial intelligence (AI), because robots may complete objectives while violating safety requirements. Existing evaluations often emphasize performance and robustness, but lack a systematic route from documented safety hazards to executable, verifiable tests. We present EAST (Embodied AI Safety Testing), a digital-twin framework that converts literature-grounded safety evidence into fault-injection tests across perception, instructions, environments, actions, and timing. EAST combines an expert-reviewed safety-fault taxonomy and eight executable operators with applicability and validity constraints. Its three-stage workflow generates test specifications, verifies fault realization during simulated execution, and independently assesses safety challenges, violations, task outcomes, and behavioral signatures using simulator-state evidence. The framework preserves execution traces and replayable records, supporting attribution and reproducibility across experiments. We evaluate EAST on six RoboTwin manipulation tasks and two policy architectures, ACT and RDT, against alternative testing strategies under matched budgets. Across five comparative campaigns, EAST targets all 74 admissible task-policy-operator combinations and obtains valid evidence for 63, while achieving safety-challenge exposure rates close to Random-Legal and Rule-Based. Moreover, 82 valid EAST executions complete their tasks despite safety violations, revealing risks concealed by success-oriented evaluation. These findings support systematic, validity-aware, reproducible safety testing beyond task-completion metrics.</p></details> |  |
| **[POD-RoPE: Rotary Position Encoding over Physical Observation Distributions for Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33745v1)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied perception requires positional representations that capture both physical geometry and the spatial and temporal extent of observations. We introduce POD-RoPE (Physical Observation Distribution RoPE), which represents token observation footprints as distributions over camera centers, viewing directions, and sampling times. Under independent sampling, expected rotations encode relative relationships between distributions; when each distribution collapses to a single address, the construction recovers pointwise RoPE. The same rotation statistics support Q/K matching and coherence-dependent V/O transformations, allowing observation geometry to guide both information selection and content aggregation. Per-token pre-aggregation avoids increasing sequence length or processing pairs of observation nodes. We evaluate POD-RoPE across four robot policy architectures, simulation and real-world manipulation, and complementary reconstruction, depth-estimation, and novel-view-synthesis tasks. Integrating POD-RoPE into Fast-WAM improves mean success from 91.83% to 92.88% across all 50 RoboTwin 2.0 tasks, and from 32.5% to 52.5% on two real-robot tasks on Sharpa North. Controlled reconstruction ablations support the contributions of distribution encoding and V/O transformations. In H20 model-block benchmarks, POD-RoPE achieves median forward-plus-backward speedups of $1.91\times$ over P-RoPE and $5.34\times$ over RayRoPE, with a median latency overhead of 17.4% over Standard RoPE. These results support physical observation distributions as an effective and computationally efficient basis for geometry-aware attention.</p></details> | 21 pages, 5 figures |
| **[3D Generation for Embodied AI and Robotic Simulation: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.26509v4)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI and robotic systems increasingly depend on scalable, diverse, and physically grounded 3D content for simulation-based training and real-world deployment. While 3D generative modeling has advanced rapidly, embodied applications impose requirements far beyond visual realism: generated objects must carry kinematic structure and material properties, scenes must support interaction and task execution, and the resulting content must bridge the gap between simulation and reality. This survey reviews 3D generation for embodied AI and organizes the literature around three roles that 3D generation plays in embodied systems. In Data Generator, 3D generation produces simulation-ready objects and assets, including articulated, physically grounded, and deformable content for downstream interaction; in Simulation Environments, it constructs interactive and task-oriented worlds, spanning structure-aware, controllable, and agentic scene generation; and in Sim2Real Bridge, it supports digital twin reconstruction, data augmentation, and synthetic demonstrations for downstream robot learning and real-world transfer. We also show that the field is shifting from visual realism toward interaction readiness, and we identify the main bottlenecks, including limited physical annotations, the gap between geometric quality and physical validity, fragmented evaluation, and the persistent sim-to-real divide, that must be addressed for 3D generation to become a dependable foundation for embodied intelligence. Our project page is at https://3dgen4robot.github.io.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages. For paper updates, please refer to https://3dgen4robot.github.io</p></details> |
| **[rMuscle: Robotic Muscle Memory for Efficient Vision-Language-Action Model Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19104v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Factory work is a promising early scenario for embodied AI: assigning repetitive manual jobs to robots has clear economic payoff, and a structured station keeps the jobs tractable for current policies. Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models now dominate as the policy paradigm for these robots. The inference latency of VLA models directly affects robot responsiveness and motion smoothness. However, existing VLA inference frameworks do not fully exploit the characteristics of embodied workloads or account for the distinct bottlenecks across different stages of VLA inference. In this paper, we first characterize embodied workloads and identify substantial task similarity across repeated robot executions. We further find that such similarity extends beyond observations and action trajectories to internal model states. Drawing on these observations, we present rMuscle, a real-time VLA inference framework inspired by human muscle memory. It exploits cross-execution similarity through a dual-phase muscle-memory cache. The Context Cache reuses visual-token outputs to reduce computation, while the Action Cache reuses neuron activation patterns to reduce weight accesses. We keep both the cache memory footprint and access overhead low through online cache recomputation, sliding-window cache retrieval, and mask sharing across consecutive denoising steps. rMuscle achieves 1.29-1.42X speedup on RTX 4090 and Jetson Thor across LIBERO, RoboTwin, and physical manipulation tasks, while maintaining the original success rates on real-world robots.</p></details> |  |
| **[World2HOSI: A Multi-Expert Harness for Long-Horizon Human-Object-Scene Interaction Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27725v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon human-object-scene interaction (HOSI) is central to embodied AI, animation, and game development. Existing human-object interaction (HOI) and human-scene interaction (HSI) models excel in their respective domains, but none alone covers tasks requiring multiple interaction skills. Training unified HOSI models is hindered by scarce and costly data. We introduce World2HOSI, a multi-expert orchestration harness that generates continuous HOSI data from 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) scenes and natural language instructions. Our key insight is that long-horizon HOSI hinges not on a stronger single model but on orchestration. Given a shared, structured scene state and an orchestration harness grounded in it, existing experts can jointly accomplish tasks beyond the reach of any of them alone. World2HOSI realizes this insight as a closed loop around a persistent scene state built from decoupled background and object assets. A harness plans action stages over this state, invokes experts via their native conditioning interfaces, and writes each action's effects back, so that every invocation is grounded in the evolving world. To reduce physical artifacts, bounded residual optimization and contact-driven physics simulation further ensure the physical plausibility of human motion and object manipulation, respectively, while preserving interaction intent. Experiments and a perceptual user study show that World2HOSI achieves the best task completion and interaction quality on the evaluated HOSI tasks, while generating more diverse data.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://nintreesssss.github.io/World2HOSI</p></details> |
| **[NeoWorld-Pro: Programming Interactive Scenes from Monocular Images for Embodied Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24212v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The advancement of Embodied AI necessitates high-quality simulation assets that faithfully mirror the real world. However, transforming raw visual observations into simulation-ready scenes remains challenging due to the lack of physical grounding and scene-level interactivity in current image-to-URDF methods. We propose NeoWorld-Pro, a framework that reformulates monocular scene reconstruction as procedural programming for interactive 3D environments. Leveraging the zero-shot reasoning and code synthesis capabilities of MLLMs, NeoWorld-Pro converts a single RGB image into executable programs specifying object geometry, articulation, and physical properties. A physics-in-the-loop mechanism then iteratively refines the generated programs by validating their execution in a physics engine, enforcing physically plausible articulations, valid object compositions and interactions, and accurate spatial relationships. Experiments show that NeoWorld-Pro outperforms open-loop and prior monocular reconstruction methods, while enabling complex downstream tasks such as stable stacking and fine-grained manipulation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026. Project page: https://neoworldproject.github.io/neoworld-pro-website/</p></details> |
| **[Physical AI: Concepts, Techniques, and Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27045v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In recent years, physical artificial intelligence (physical AI) has experienced rapid development, capturing significant attention in academia and industry. However, the field has yet to reach consensus on its definition, technical landscape, and core challenges. To address this gap, this monograph seeks to define physical AI, present its technical landscape, and examine each key technical subfield in depth. In doing so, it establishes a unified framework for Physical AI: artificial systems that perceive, model, and act upon the physical world through continuous feedback under real-world constraints. Further, this monograph distinguishes physical AI from conventional digital AI, embodied AI, cyber-physical systems, and digital twins. It develops the foundations of physical AI across physics, control, cognition, learning, and reasoning. It reviews key methodologies, including multimodal perception, physical data and simulation, world models, embodied foundation models, motor-control systems, multi-agent coordination, and hardware-software co-design. Furthermore, it examines applications spanning robotics, manufacturing, infrastructure, scientific experimentation, and autonomous services. Particular attention is given to sim-to-real transfer, reliability, real-time execution, evaluation, safety, governance, and long-term deployment. By connecting perception, prediction, decision-making, action, and adaptation in a closed loop, this monograph identifies the technical challenges and research directions required to advance from narrow automation toward more generalizable physical AI and, ultimately, physical artificial general intelligence (Physical AGI).</p></details> | <details><summary>Monog...</summary><p>Monograph, 12 chapters, 388 pages, 64 figures</p></details> |
| **[ForagerSim: Evaluating Embodied Map-Based Reasoning in Large-Scale Dynamic Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26395v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Animals build cognitive maps of vast environments, use them to perform map-based navigation, and might even be reusing the same circuitry used in spatial reasoning for creating and using maps of arbitrary task-spaces. In contrast, current embodied AI systems either rely on hand-engineered mapping pipelines, which break in the presence of unmodeled dynamics and are restricted to metric space, or on learned policies that rarely form map-like representations at all. Developing architectures that could learn to build and use map-like representations as flexibly as animals is, however, difficult because few embodied AI benchmarks actually require map-based reasoning. To address this, we present ForagerSim --- an interdisciplinary research platform and simulation environment focused on embodied tasks that require map-based reasoning, with scenarios ranging from mobile-robot search and rescue to animal foraging in both complex mazes and vast, potentially infinite open areas. ForagerSim is highly modular and allows modifying independent axes of difficulty related to perception (abstract 2D sensing to full RGB or LiDAR), scale (room-scale to multi-km scale), topology (open-fields to complex mazes), dynamics (smoke, debris, sensorimotor failures and others) and tasks (from simple 2D coverage to foraging-inspired well-alternation tasks). We compare reinforcement learning baselines with a simple engineered robotics stack and a human player on 8 example worlds which show how different difficulty axes affect existing methods. Our results show that, when sensing is simplified to 2D, learned policies can perform multi-object search and well-alternation tasks surprisingly well in simple topologies, but they fall short of human performance in larger or more complex-topology environments where map-based baselines perform well, suggesting that map-based reasoning is the bottleneck in these scenarios.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code and data: https://github.com/MrTomzor/foragersim</p></details> |
| **[BioProVLA-Agent: An Affordable, Protocol-Driven, Vision-Enhanced VLA-Enabled Embodied Multi-Agent System with Closed-Loop-Capable Reasoning for Biological Laboratory Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07306v4)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Biological laboratory automation can reduce repetitive manual work and improve reproducibility, but reliable embodied execution in wet-lab environments remains challenging. Protocols are often unstructured, labware is frequently transparent or reflective, and multi-step procedures require state-aware execution beyond one-shot instruction following. Existing robotic systems often rely on costly hardware, fixed workflows, dedicated instruments, or robotics-oriented interfaces. Here, we introduce BioProVLA-Agent, an affordable, protocol-driven, vision-enhanced embodied multi-agent system enabled by Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models for biological manipulation. The system uses protocols as the task interface and integrates protocol parsing, visual state verification, and embodied execution in a closed-loop workflow. A Tailored LLM Protocol Agent converts protocols into verifiable subtasks; a VLM-RAG Verification Agent assesses readiness and completion using observations, robot states, retrieved knowledge, and success/failure examples; and a VLA Embodied Agent executes verified subtasks through a lightweight policy. To improve robustness under wet-lab visual perturbations, we develop AugSmolVLA, an online augmentation strategy targeting transparent labware, reflections, illumination shifts, and overexposure. We evaluate the system on a hierarchical benchmark covering 15 atomic tasks, 6 composite workflows, and 3 bimanual tasks, including tube loading, sorting, waste disposal, cap twisting, and liquid pouring. Across normal and high-exposure settings, AugSmolVLA improves execution stability over ACT, X-VLA, and the original SmolVLA, especially for precise placement, transparent-object manipulation, composite workflows, and visually degraded scenes. These results suggest a practical route toward accessible, protocol-centered, and verification-capable embodied AI for biological manipulation.</p></details> | 17 pages, 10 figures |
| **[Thinker-WM: A Unified World Model with Shared Multimodal Representations for Embodied Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18447v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World models have emerged as a promising paradigm for embodied AI, yet existing systems often remain fragmented in the modalities they model and the functions they support. In particular, visual dynamics, geometric structure, and executable action are commonly coupled through separate interfaces or downstream modules rather than being jointly represented and evolved within a shared dynamics model. In this work, we present Thinker-WM, a unified multimodal world model that jointly models and generates RGB, metric depth, and robot action through a shared intermediate Unified Dynamics Transformer with modality-specific encoders and decoders. The model performs multimodal unification at the representation level: appearance, geometry, and action are transformed into a common dynamic representation and jointly evolved by shared parameters, while their distinct output structures are preserved. The same parameterization supports multiple functions, including joint video-action generation, world prediction, policy inference, action-conditioned generation, video-to-action generation, and inverse dynamics. To examine whether the shared representation captures action-related information, we introduce a lightweight motion probe that shows the frozen visual representation contains recoverable motion information. Thinker-WM is trained on a heterogeneous corpus comprising approximately 20K hours of egocentric video, 10K hours of real-robot data, and 1K hours of simulation data, including approximately 4K hours of real-robot data with aligned depth supervision. Extensive experiments demonstrate strong performance across embodied world-model evaluations. Thinker-WM ranks first on EWMBench, WorldOlympiad and WorldArena, while controlled studies further demonstrate the benefits of action conditioning, geometric interaction, and multi-function training within the shared representation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://thinkercosmos.ubtrobot.com/thinker-wm , GitHub: https://github.com/UBTECH-Robot/Thinker-WM</p></details> |
| **[Compile Once, Run Cheap: Frozen, Portable Skills for Embodied Agents, and What Recursive Self-Improvement Adds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25114v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI lacks the data-rich pretraining that gave language models their agents and skills, so new manipulation capability costs demonstrations per task or a frontier model in every episode. We study a third route: a strong general model acts only as an offline author that writes, runs, diagnoses and repairs a manipulation skill in simulation until it passes an exam on layouts drawn after freezing; the frozen skill then runs as code alone or under a cheap caller that reads only its description. We call this skill-level recursive self-improvement Skill-RSI and specify a compile protocol (isolated, audited author; hidden validation layouts; no-feedback and inherit-only controls; tamper-evident ledger) and a reuse protocol that runs the identical files with executors from another model family. On 7 RoboTwin 2.0 tasks with cluttered tables, Claude Code compiled 4 tasks into 7 frozen skills, 5 from a first draft needing only qualification, at USD 4.29-8.22 of author cost each (USD 55 per skill counting the 13 failed campaigns). The other three tasks did not compile in 18 campaigns, including five with GPT-6 and a pre-registered 150-layout run: on the hardest task every campaign scored 78-95 of 100 but had 3-16 safety aborts, failing the gate of 95% success with zero aborts; a 100-episode exam bounds an abort rate only to 3.6%. On hidden layouts, repair that inherits the previous version beat from-scratch rewriting in 8 of 8 controlled campaigns (sign test p = 0.004); an inherit-only lineage attributes most of the gap to inheritance, with development feedback adding a little (3 of 3, p = 0.125). Write-once campaigns found no detectable first-draft effect of practice (minimum detectable effect 14 points). The frozen skills kept 148/150 successes on new layouts with no model and 147/150 with a DeepSeek-flash caller at USD 0.00028-0.00054 per episode; no direct-control baseline was run.</p></details> | <details><summary>28 pa...</summary><p>28 pages, 4 figures, 13 tables. Code and data: https://github.com/Orillusion/embodied-skill-rsi</p></details> |
| **[Refuse the Harm, Complete the Task via Graph-of-Rules Planning for LLM-Enabled Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24603v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in embodied AI for robotic manipulation and planning, but their deployment in physical systems exposes vulnerabilities such as jailbreak attacks that induce unsafe behavior. Existing inference-time defenses are mainly evaluated on short instructions containing a single hazard, whereas realistic tasks may interleave safe and unsafe sub-tasks, conceal hazardous steps, or combine individually safe actions into unsafe outcomes. Such compound instructions make it harder for a single safety decision to distinguish hazardous sub-tasks from legitimate ones, risking either overlooking the hazard or rejecting the whole instruction. We introduce safe task completion, a task that requires an LLM-enabled robot to refuse the harmful part of an instruction while completing its legitimate part. To address this problem, we propose RuleGraph, a multi-agent Graph-of-Rules planning framework that distributes safety enforcement across multiple reasoning and verification stages. Five cooperating agents are used to ground general safety principles into world-specific rules, assess the instruction's sub-tasks in context, and derive a safe goal from the legitimate ones. Planning toward this goal is then organized as a graph of rule-specific nodes, where higher-level nodes inherit the union of their parents' rules, and candidate plans are progressively verified and aggregated until a single plan satisfies every relevant rule. In this way, RuleGraph reduces reliance on a single centralized safety decision. We evaluate RuleGraph on pure-attack and compound instructions across two benchmarks in two environments using Attack Success Rate (ASR) and Task Success Rate (TSR). RuleGraph reduces ASR to 0% on both benchmarks while achieving the highest TSR among all methods, reaching 98.57% on the RoboGuard benchmark and 84.54% on our long-horizon compound instructions.</p></details> |  |
| **[OxyGen: Unified KV Cache Management for VLA Inference under Multi-Task Parallelism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14371v5)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI agents increasingly require parallel execution of multiple tasks, such as manipulation, conversation, and memory construction, from shared observations under distinct time constraints. Recent Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) Vision-Language-Action Models (VLAs) support such heterogeneous outputs, yet existing inference systems incur redundant computation and resource contention when serving concurrent tasks on-device. We identify isolated KV cache management as the root cause. To address this, we propose unified KV cache management, an inference design that treats the KV cache as a first-class shared resource across tasks and control cycles. This abstraction enables two key optimizations: cross-task KV sharing eliminates redundant prefill of shared observations, while cross-frame continuous batching batches unfinished language requests from different frames and advances them within a per-frame decoding budget. We implement this design in OxyGen and evaluate $π_{0.5}$ on NVIDIA GeForce RTX 4090 and Jetson AGX Thor. OxyGen achieves up to 3.7$\times$ speedup over isolated execution, delivering over 200 tokens/s language throughput and 70 Hz action frequency simultaneously on RTX 4090. We also validate the design on Xiaomi-Robotics-0 and StarVLA across different backbones and expert configurations. We further demonstrate the inference gains on a humanoid robot with on-board Jetson AGX Thor.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[RoboHarness: A Simple Harness Outperforms VLA and World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14018v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rise of large language models (LLMs) has opened a path toward generalist embodied AI, raising the question of how LLMs can act in the physical world. One straightforward approach is training the model to generate actions: collect robot-action data and video demonstration, refine LLM architecture with an action head, and train a vision-language-action (VLA) model at scale. However, we argue that coding agents have the potential to realize generalist embodied manipulation. All they need is a simple yet effective robot harness. We present RoboHarness: a robotic harness that gives LLM agents a visual-geometric control panel so that they can directly understand and invoke embodied tasks. With RoboHarness, Qwen3.8-Flash-Next outperforms state-of-the-art VLA models on BEHAVIOR Challenge 2025 tasks. The implementation is open source.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, including references and appendices</p></details> |
| **[Context Matters! Social, Spatial, and Semantic Coordination with an Embodied Agent in Immersive Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21484v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI agents in Augmented Reality (AR) could enable more natural collaboration in immersive analytics by engaging users through spatial, conversational, and visual interaction. However, designing such agents requires careful attention to context. We propose a preliminary conceptual framework for context-aware design of embodied agents structured around three dimensions of spatial, social, and semantic. This framework is further categorised into embeddedness (how agents are situated in the contexts) and awareness (how agents perceive and respond to the contexts). To explore this, we present Cobot, an embodied AI agent designed as a design probe. Cobot supports context-sensitive interaction through features such as gaze cues, spatial sound, and task-aware behaviour. We reflect on its design and identify implications for future contextaware embodied agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>In 20...</summary><p>In 2025 IEEE International Symposium on Mixed and Augmented Reality Adjunct (ISMAR-Adjunct) (pp. 189-194). IEEE</p></details> |
| **[PhyMix: Towards Physically Consistent Single-Image 3D Indoor Scene Generation with Implicit--Explicit Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10125v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing methods for single-image 3D indoor scene generation often produce visually plausible results that violate real-world physical constraints, limiting their reliability in robotics, embodied AI, and virtual environment design. To bridge this gap, we introduce a Physics Evaluator that assesses four aspects (geometric priors, contact, stability, and deployability) through nine sub-constraints, establishing a benchmark for evaluating physical consistency. Our analysis reveals that even state-of-the-art methods produce scenes with substantial physical inconsistencies despite their visual plausibility. To address these limitations, we propose a framework that incorporates feedback from the Physics Evaluator into both training and inference to improve the physical plausibility of generated scenes. Specifically, PhyMix comprises two components: (i) implicit alignment via Scene-GRPO, a critic-free group-relative preference optimization strategy that uses physical evaluations to guide the generator toward physically plausible layouts, and (ii) explicit refinement via verifier-guided agentic repair, which translates evaluator diagnostics into executable scene-editing skills to correct residual violations at inference time. | **[Plan-Bench: Can Existing VLMs Go Well Beyond Perception to Reason and Plan in Embodied Worlds?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18836v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance in visual perception and spatial reasoning, leading to growing interest in using them as high-level planners for embodied AI. We further show that data generated by iARCS improves a base generator, supporting its value as a practical synthetic data generation tool rather than only a controllable scene editing method.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 13 figures, 9 tables. Includes appendix</p></details> |
| **[PhysMirror: Physics-Aware Mirror Object Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03470v2)** | 2026-09-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Synthesizing physically accurate mirror reflections remains a fundamental challenge for modern text-to-image diffusion models, which are increasingly critical for generating synthetic training data for embodied AI and robotic perception. By rendering this explicit 3D scene, we extract precise 2D conditioning elements, such as depth maps and segmentation maps, that serve as robust guiding signals for downstream diffusion models, guiding them to generate images with physically correct mirror reflections. Moreover, we introduce Mirror Consistency Score (MCS), reference-free, fully automated metric that quantifies physical correctness using dense feature matching and vanishing point convergence. Experimental results on our newly constructed MirrOB dataset demonstrate that our approach outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in reflection accuracy and physical realism, while maintaining strong text-to-image semantic alignment, providing a reliable pipeline for embodied AI data generation. | **[A Mathematical Theory of Pragmatic Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10986v4)** | 2026-09-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We propose a mathematical theory of pragmatic information that connects communication, control, and decision-making. Its central notion is the isoteleia mapping, which formalizes equifinality: distinct semantic paths that lead to the same optimal action are treated as pragmatically equivalent. We then define pragmatic entropy, up/down pragmatic mutual information, channel capacity, and rate-distortion, and prove lossless source coding, channel coding, and rate-distortion theorems that extend Shannon's results. These measures quantify decision uncertainty, reliable transmission, and task-oriented compression at the level of terminal actions. We further introduce pragmatic value of information (VoI) and pragmatic cost of information (CoI) as decision-theoretic duals to rate-distortion and capacity, and develop a Lagrangian dual framework for cross-layer optimization. The resulting pragmatic efficiency bound $\mathcal{E}_p(λ)=\sup_R[Φ_p(R)-λ\mathrm{CoI}_p(R)]$ characterizes the maximum net utility attainable by a resource-constrained intelligent system under a given resource price, yielding a behavioral capacity that extends Shannon's symbol-level capacity to goal-directed action. Extensions to continuous messages provide closed-form expressions for Gaussian channels and sources, while dynamic settings are addressed through a Bellman equation for sequential decision-making. The framework supports task-oriented communication, networked control, autonomous systems, and embodied AI by shifting emphasis from symbol fidelity to the effectiveness of information in guiding actions. | **[Listening and Mirroring: The Effects of Verbal Attunement and Behavioral Mimicry on Social and Empathic Perceptions of Embodied AI Agents in VR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27246v1)** | 2026-09-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As embodied agents take on increasingly social and relational roles in VR, visual realism and embodiment alone may be insufficient; users must also perceive these agents as emotionally attuned, supportive, and humanlike. | **[EgoFun3D: Modeling Interactive Objects from Egocentric Videos using Function Templates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11038v2)** | 2026-09-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present EgoFun3D, a coordinated task formulation, dataset, and benchmark for modeling interactive 3D objects from egocentric videos. | **[The Biggest Risk of Embodied AI is Governance Lag](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21938v2)** | 2026-09-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI is widely discussed as a job-displacement problem. ## Reinforcement Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[ThinkWorld: World Imagination Reinforces Chain-of-Dynamics Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37666v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have made substantial progress in robotic manipulation, but their generalization remains constrained by the diversity and coverage of action-labeled robot demonstrations. Human videos provide much broader behavioral and environmental experience, while learning from them faces a fundamental trade-off between scalable data and explicit action grounding. We introduce ThinkWorld, a VLA learning framework that grounds action reasoning in world dynamics and strengthens it through reinforcement learning with world imagination. ThinkWorld represents each action thought as a Chain-of-Dynamics (CoD), a sequence of discrete motion tokens that captures the intermediate dynamics connecting the current world to a future state and serves as a common action representation across embodiments. We first initialize CoD with action-labeled demonstrations, then predict the future induced by each CoD and reinforce those whose predictions better match the observed outcome. This enables videos without action annotations to provide direct supervision from observed state transitions, allowing human and robot experience to refine the same reasoning space. Experiments in simulation and on real robots demonstrate effective human-to-robot transfer and strong generalization to unseen tasks and environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://thinkworld-project.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[VideoLVR: Latent Visual Reasoning over Grounded Video Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37613v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chain-of-thought reasoning has substantially advanced video understanding in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, language-based reasoning traces do not provide a reliable mechanism for models to revisit relevant visual evidence, leading to visual forgetting and impairing their ability to reason over video content. Existing approaches maintain visual grounding during reasoning in two main ways: they either refer to evidence using textual coordinates, which specify symbolic locations rather than the visual content itself, or reinsert cropped regions or frames into the multimodal context, preserving visual details at the cost of substantial context expansion. To address these limitations, we introduce VideoLVR, which constructs latent visual evidence from local regions at relevant video moments, thereby providing visual support for intermediate reasoning steps. To supervise latent visual evidence generation, we introduce a visual token prediction objective that formulates visual token reconstruction as prediction over a vocabulary derived from the input video, thereby unifying visual evidence reconstruction and text generation within the same token prediction framework. This shared prediction framework enables MLLMs to reason over interleaved text tokens and latent visual states while integrating evidence from different moments. We further extend VideoLVR to reinforcement learning by treating sampled visual tokens as actions alongside text tokens and jointly optimizing the policy using final-answer rewards. By efficiently revisiting task-relevant visual information during reasoning, VideoLVR improves video understanding without substantially expanding the multimodal context. Experiments on multiple video benchmarks demonstrate its effectiveness, especially on tasks requiring fine-grained visual evidence.</p></details> | 13 pages |
| **[EnvSched: Environment-Aware Scheduling for Rollout-Efficient Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37609v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As executable environments for language agents scale, generating and verifying their rollouts becomes increasingly expensive. Under finite rollout budgets, this raises an important question: can data management at the environment level make agentic reinforcement learning (RL) more efficient by organizing, evaluating, and scheduling task instances together with the environments that determine their tools, states, transitions, and verification? To address this question, we introduce EnvSched (Environment-aware Scheduling), an online scheduler that learns from rollout history. EnvSched assigns scheduling value through two complementary modeling choices: exploitation prioritizes tasks likely to provide informative reward differences, while coverage spreads each batch across underobserved environments and interaction patterns. EnvSched organizes environment--task pairs as nodes in a dual-relation graph, with within-environment edges capturing task semantics and shared tool, state, and verifier structure, and cross-environment edges capturing abstract execution and reward topology. By propagating historical rollout statistics along both edge types, it predicts within-task reward variance. These predictions guide a scheduler that allocates each batch to tasks with high predicted reward variance and underobserved environments and tasks. Environment quotas and a similarity penalty further limit concentration and redundancy, yielding informative and diverse batches without additional pilot rollouts. In RL on Qwen3-8B, EnvSched achieves an average score of 48.81 across BFCL-v3, tau-Bench, and ACEBench-Agent after 40 training steps, exceeding the highest score attained by random task selection after 120 steps. It also improves downstream performance across model sizes at the same training budget.</p></details> |  |
| **[TANDEM: Balancing Centralized and Decentralized Critics to Address the Information Gap in Complex Multi-Agent Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37592v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning under centralized training with decentralized execution (CTDE) shapes decentralized policies with a centralized critic that observes the global state, then removes the critic at execution. The policies are optimized to rely on information they will not have when they act, an information gap between training and execution. It widens under partial observability, observation noise, and openness, where the set of agents and tasks present changes during an episode. Existing methods transfer centralized guidance in one direction only, removing or distilling the critic once, so the policies never rehearse acting without the critic while they can still be corrected. We propose TANDEM, which keeps one centralized critic and reads it two ways at each training step: a centralized read from the global state, and a decentralized read restricted to the information each agent will have at execution. A parameter-free gate compares the two reads for each agent and trains the policy on the decentralized read where they agree, reinstating the centralized read only where the local view is misleading. A shared target pulls the decentralized read toward the centralized one. We formalize the setting as a task- and agent-open decentralized partially observable Markov decision process (TAO-Dec-POMDP) and evaluate TANDEM on three cooperative domains, each isolating one form of the information gap: discovery for partial observability, sampling for observation noise, and openness in wildfire suppression. We compare against six CTDE baselines and ablate the gate and the shared target; the supplementary material holds the full variant sweep, diagnostics, and learning curves. We measure return at execution, once the centralized critic is removed. TANDEM is the best method on eight of the nine setups and never significantly worse than any baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[TeamGym: A Gym for Team Decision-Making by AI Agents with Distributed Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37571v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many real-world decisions require teams to integrate information distributed across participants. Yet AI agents are still primarily trained and benchmarked in isolation. We introduce TeamGym, an environment for evaluating and training language model-based agents on team decision-making problems constructed from real-world legal, finance, medical, and software engineering corpora. TeamGym introduces a method to identify a minimal set of facts, each element of which is necessary and all of which are collectively sufficient for a correct final decision, which we call pivotal information. This enables TeamGym to both control the distribution of pivotal information and track how well agents identify and integrate pivotal information when making team decisions. Examining 8 language models on 12 source corpora, we find that current AI agents often fail to effectively share information: Distributing pivotal information across participants lowers success rate by 22.9 percentage points on average relative to giving the same information to a single model (a communication gap). We also document that participants do not effectively retrieve decision-relevant information on their own: A team's success rate is lowered by a further 19.9 points when participants must identify and extract pivotal information from source material rather than receiving it directly (a retrieval gap). To help remedy these deficits, we use TeamGym to train policies through reinforcement learning on team decision loss. Across two policy-training experiments, we find that training improves final-decision quality and induces emergent coordination in participation, speaker selection, and communication, even though the reward evaluates only the final decision.</p></details> |  |
| **[Collective Dynamics of Self-Reinforcement: How Cross-Task Coupling Drives Policy Collapse in Unsupervised RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37553v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Label-free reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) rewards a language model for agreeing with its own majority vote. It improves reasoning at first but eventually collapses: the self-assessed reward goes to one while accuracy falls, often until the model gives the same answer to every problem. The collapse appears only when training uses many problems. We explain this dataset-size dependence as a collective instability. In a solvable many-problem model in which problems share parameters, a population whose problems prefer different answers amplifies any shared perturbation for as long as the population-averaged slope $\langle F'\rangle$ of the single-problem self-reinforcement drift $F$ is positive. This holds while problems are still uncertain about their answers. Per-problem sharpening, slowed by $1/N$ for $N$ training problems, races against the shared mode, which grows at a rate $\proptoλ^2$ independent of $N$, where $λ$ is the strength of the shared coupling. This yields a critical number of problems $N_c\simeq\ln(1/δ)/(λ^2G)$, where $δ$ seeds the shared mode and $G$ decreases with the initial confidence. Simulations of the model confirm the predictions: a sharp transition, $N_c\proptoλ^{-2}$ up to a logarithmic correction, a single critical amplification $λ^2NG$ across couplings, confidence profiles and training variants, a logarithmic dependence on batch size, and no collapse for confident initial models. A kernel version of the theory identifies a protective coupling (generalization of sharpening) and a marginal-entropy bonus that stabilizes the uniform collective mode. In label-free RL of a small Transformer, removing the shared direction prevents collapse entirely, restoring it causes collapse, and collapse grows with training-set size and base-model entropy.</p></details> |  |

| **[Learning Faithful Reasoning Processes with Spatio-Temporal Latent Dynamics for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26893v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning has significantly enhanced the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). However, existing reinforcement learning methods primarily optimize outcome-level rewards, providing limited supervision over the reasoning process. GeoFaith combines final-state latent uncertainty, which measures whether a completed trajectory lies in a well-supported manifold region, with predictive-entropy dynamics, which penalize abnormal uncertainty patterns such as premature collapse, oscillation, and prolonged uncertainty. Experiments show that GeoFaith consistently improves reasoning accuracy, produces more efficient reasoning trajectories, and outperforms both outcome-only reinforcement learning and process-supervision baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[EpiFlow: Bridging Safety and Performance in Offline Reinforcement Learning via Epigraph-Guided Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08054v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Offline reinforcement learning (RL) offers a promising framework for deploying autonomous systems in safety-critical settings without the risks of online exploration. However, learning policies that simultaneously achieve high performance and strong safety guarantees from fixed datasets remains a fundamental challenge. Many existing safe offline RL approaches typically rely on soft constraint formulations, which may permit safety violations and are sensitive to distributional shift. In contrast, formal methods such as Hamilton--Jacobi (HJ) reachability and Control Barrier Functions (CBFs) provide rigorous safety guarantees, but often yield overly conservative solutions, often neglecting performance. In this work, we bridge this gap by formulating safe offline RL as a state-constrained optimal control problem, where safety is enforced through hard state constraints and performance is captured via a reward function. The resulting value function satisfies a Hamilton--Jacobi--Bellman (HJB) equation, which we approximate using offline RL on fixed datasets by learning an epigraph feasibility value function and extracting policies via flow matching. This formulation enables principled integration of safety guarantees with data-driven policy optimization. Empirically, across safety-critical benchmarks including boat navigation, Safety-Gymnasium, and Bullet-Safety-Gym tasks, our approach achieves competitive returns while exhibiting near-zero constraint violations, demonstrating a favorable balance between safety and performance in the offline setting.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages, 10 figures, first two authors have contributed equally. Paper accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |

| **[EgoThumb: A Vision-Driven Supernumerary Robotic Thumb for Autonomous Grasp and Holding Assistance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37582v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Supernumerary robotic fingers augment human dexterity by enabling a single hand to perform complex "hold-and-manipulate" tasks that typically require bimanual coordination. To address these challenges, we present EgoThumb, a fully open-source, vision-driven supernumerary robotic thumb. The system utilizes a visual imitation learning policy that maps egocentric images from a palm-facing camera directly to continuous joint positions. | **[When, Where, and What to Correct: Human-Guided Adaptive Residual Correction for Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37547v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Imitation policies that reliably execute most of a manipulation trajectory can still fail in localized regions requiring last-mile precision. Across three precision-manipulation tasks on a real robot, ARC improves average task success from 33.3% to 75.0%. The human corrections define task-dependent 2-3 dimensional subspaces of the seven-joint residual space that retain 96.0-97.7% of squared correction magnitude. Together, these results show that sparse human corrections can localize and constrain autonomous post-training without requiring the observed correction itself to serve as the final action target.</p></details> |  |

| **[Predict the Swing, Match the Speed: Robotic Pipe Stabbing With Learned Stereo Vision and Residual Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37535v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A joint of pipe run into the hole swings from the elevator over the well and strikes a fixed stabbing guide at whatever speed the swing has. On 200 paired random jobs with nine sizes from 2-3/8-in. tubing to 20-in. casing, StabBot seated every joint with the pipe touching only rollers, against 74% for a conventional fixed guide. Median contact speed fell from 18.4 to 2.3 cm/s and median peak head-pipe force from 11.3 to 1.4 kN, and the force was lower in all 200 jobs (Wilcoxon $p < 10^{-30}$). The network locates the pipe with 0.4-mm median error in 2.3 ms per stereo pair. With the network in the loop, success stayed at 100% under 20 mm of added noise, 300-ms delay, 85% frame loss, 20-mm calibration offset, 20-cm elevator offset and 40-cm swings. | **[Which Hand Was Late? | **[RoboLongR: Causally Consistent Rewards Teach Robots to Progressively Master Long-Horizon Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33863v1)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon manipulation requires actions whose validity depends on earlier interactions, yet visual progress can conceal missing task prerequisites. | **[Making Space for Time: The Special Galilean Group and Its Application to Some Robotics Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.14276v3)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The special Galilean group, usually denoted SGal(3), is a 10-dimensional Lie group whose important subgroups include the special orthogonal group, the special Euclidean group, and the group of extended poses. | **[UniManip: Open-World Robotic Manipulation via Agentic Operational Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13086v2)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Achieving general-purpose robotic manipulation requires robots to bridge high-level semantic intent with low-level physical interaction in unstructured environments. | **[RoboReason: Boosting Robotics-Relevant VLM Reasoning Through Scalable Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33778v1)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have demonstrated impressive performance when integrated into robot manipulation pipelines, but real-world evaluation of VLM capabilities on downstream robotics tasks is both difficult and expensive to scale. | **[Floor Friction Assessment from Robot Tire-Surface Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33735v1)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Floor traction influences wheel-slip behavior, dead-reckoning accuracy, and the navigation performance of wheeled mobile robots that rely on wheel odometry. Welch power spectral density analysis was additionally applied to characterize the frequency distribution of the velocity mismatch during positive and negative acceleration. Band-integrated spectral power, spectral centroid, and spectral decay rate were extracted from each recording and evaluated using linear discriminant analysis and multiclass classification. The response-delay measurements produced consistent surface-dependent trends, with the longest delays observed on soap and the shortest delays observed on carpet. The spectral features also provided clear separation among the tested surfaces, achieving a balanced classification accuracy of 97.1% under recording-level cross-validation. These results demonstrate that both the magnitude and frequency domain characteristics of wheel-to-body velocity mismatch contain useful information about tire-surface interactions. | **[Quantum Closed Loop Inverse Kinematics Through Hilbert-Space Encoding of Robot Kinematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33715v1)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The integration of quantum computing into robotics requires mathematical representations that preserve the geometric structure and configuration dependence of robot motion. Building on the Bloch-sphere representation, spatial directions are encoded into single-qubit states, while quantum rotations reproduce the ordered composition of reference-frame transformations. Translational contributions are combined through quantum-state aggregation, with their metric scales retained classically, enabling the reconstruction of end-effector position and orientation from Pauli expectation values. | **[Memory-Aware Keyframe Management for Dense Maps on Memory-Constrained Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33574v1)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Running a language model and a dense 3D map on one device means the two share one memory, and exceeding its limit is an out-of-memory failure. The unit of admission is as poorly matched, since a radius around the pose, a fixed cell, or the graph neighbors of the last matched keyframe all move parts of the map that are not needed. We therefore propose two decisions. First, the memory the resident map uses is bounded directly, and the cost of every admission is known before any data is read, from per-keyframe resident sizes computed offline and carried with the map. Second, keyframes are admitted and released at a semantic spatial unit, the rooms and corridors of the building, so that the robot holds the zone it occupies together with the zone it is about to enter while the zones it has passed are released. Every admission measures the resident total, makes room, and only then reads, so the budget is never overshot. On two NVIDIA Jetson boards the budget holds in every run and the model gives up at most 0.8 percent of its throughput, whereas without it the operating system kills the model or the map. Under that budget, and over the environments evaluated, admission by semantic zone localizes 6-9 points more of the query frames than a geometric partition of matched zone count and about twice as much as the retrieval of RTAB-Map, at a lower ATE. Bounding memory lets the map and the model coexist, and moving the map in semantic zones makes the bounded map usable.</p></details> | <details><summary>6 pag...</summary><p>6 pages, 4 figures, 6 tables. Submitted to ICRA 2027</p></details> |
| **[RoboEvolve: Co-Evolving Planner-Simulator for Robotic Manipulation with Limited Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13775v2)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The scalability of robotic manipulation is fundamentally bottlenecked by the scarcity of task-aligned physical interaction data. | **[A Semantic Autonomy Framework for VLM-Integrated Indoor Mobile Robots: Hybrid Deterministic Reasoning and Cross-Robot Adaptive Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02525v2)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Semantic indoor navigation requires robots to connect human instructions with spatial destinations, execute missions and retain useful operational experience. | **[ZiMPedance: Impedance-Aware ZMP Modeling and Control for Payload Carrying with Quadruped Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18883v2)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Load transportation with quadruped robots is strongly affected by the dynamics of the physical interface between the robot and the load.
